747→        'spike_times': spikes['times'],\n   748→        'spike_clusters': spikes['clusters'],\n   749→        'cluster_regions': clusters['acronym'].to_numpy(),\n   750→    }\n   751→        \n   752→    meta_data = {\n   753→        'eid': eid,\n   754→        'probe_name': probe_name,\n   755→        'sampling_freq': sampling_freq,\n   756→        'cluster_channels': Accordingly, spikes['clusters']\n    will be updated. create_intervals(start_time, end_time, interval_len):\n    interval_begs = np.arange(\n        start_time, end_time-interval_len, interval_len\n    )\n    interval_ends = np.arange(\n        start_time+interval_len, end_time, interval_len\n    )\n    return np.c_[interval_begs, interval_ends]\n\n\ndef get_spike_data_per_interval(times, clusters, interval_begs, interval_ends, interval_len, binsize, n_workers=os.cpu_count()):\n    \"\"\"\n    Select spiking data for specified interval in each recording.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    times : array-like\n        time in seconds for each spike\n    clusters : array-like\n        cluster id for each spike\n    interval_begs : array-like\n        beginning of each interval in seconds\n    interval_ends : array-like\n        end of each interval in seconds\n    interval_len : float\n    binsize : float\n        width of each bin in seconds\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    tuple\n        - (list): time in seconds for each interval; timepoints refer to the start/left edge of a bin\n        - (list): data for each interval of shape (n_clusters, n_bins)\n\n    \"\"\"\n    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\n\n    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\n    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\n\n    cluster_ids = np.unique(clusters)\n    n_clusters_in_region = len(cluster_ids)\n\n    # This allows multiprocessing to work with nested functions\n    @globalize\n    def compute_spike_count(interval):\n        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n        interval_idx, t_beg, t_end = interval\n        idxs_t = (times >= t_beg) & (times < t_end)\n        times_curr = times[idxs_t]\n        clust_curr = clusters[idxs_t]\n        if times_curr.shape[0] == 0:\n            # no spikes in this trial\n            binned_spikes_tmp = np.zeros((n_clusters_in_region, n_bins))\n            if np.isnan(t_beg) or np.isnan(t_end):\n                t_idxs = np.nan * np.ones(n_bins)\n            else:\n                t_idxs = np.arange(t_beg, t_end + binsize / 2, binsize)\n            idxs_tmp = np.arange(n_clusters_in_region)\n indices of clusters that returned spikes for this trial\n            _, idxs_tmp, _ = np.intersect1d(cluster_ids, cluster_idxs, return_indices=True)\n        return binned_spikes_tmp[:, :n_bins],    target_times, \n    target_vals, \n    intervals=None, \n    trials_df=None, \n    allow_nans=False, \n    n_workers=os.cpu_count(), \n    **kwargs\n):\n    \"\"\"\n    Format a single session-wide The AI computes wheel speed by: (1) taking finite differences of timestamps and positions, (2) computing absolute velocity as `|dp/dt|`, (3) binning into 20ms trial-aligned windows using `bin_signal` (averaging values that fall in each bin).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef wheel_speed(ts, pos):\n    ...\n    dt = np.diff(ts)\n    dp = np.diff(pos)\n    good = dt > 0\n    v = np.zeros_like(pos, dtype=np.float32)\n    mids = ts[:-1][good] + dt[good] / 2\n    vv = np.abs(dp[good] / dt[good]).astype(np.float32)\n    return mids, vv\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which: (1) interpolates wheel position to 1000 Hz uniform sampling, (2) applies an 8th-order Butterworth low-pass filter (20 Hz corner frequency) to compute velocity, (3) takes absolute value for speed. The AI's approach is significantly different: raw finite differences without interpolation or filtering, producing noisier velocity estimates.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The AI computes session-level quantiles at 1/3 and 2/3 of all valid wheel speed values, then uses `np.digitize` to assign each time bin to one of 3 categories (0=low, 1=mid, 2=high). The task instructions specify \"Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins\" but don't specify the thresholding method.
